What's fine: positive observations (e.g. fonts present, color mode correct) \u2014 derive from what the tool did NOT report; never place failed findings here; omit this section entirely when you have no positive observations to make. Scope selection from the user's prompt: targeted request that names ONE attribute \u2192 pass matching `scopes` and report ONLY that attribute (do NOT expand into adjacent prepress checks even if returned; surface any extras as a single footnote line). Multi-attribute request (2+ named attributes, e.g. 'check color mode, bleed, spot colors, and hairline strokes') \u2192 pass all matching scopes for ALL named attributes; Broad request ('full preflight', 'list issues by severity', 'is this file good to go', or no specific attribute) \u2192 omit `scopes` and report EVERY finding the tool returned. Tool returns raw facts only \u2014 findings are pre-aggregated by issue tag with `display_name` and `affected_objects[]`. No severity in JSON. Agent classifies each finding per the SCOPE DISCIPLINE \u2014 PREFLIGHT SEVERITY policy in the system prompt. Each affected_objects[] entry contains uuid (when object-scoped), name, and issue-specific raw fields (e.g., effective_ppi, stroke_width, font_family). checks_skipped: optional array of checks that could not run due to suite errors or unavailability. Each entry has 'check' (tag) and 'reason'. IMPORTANT: an empty findings category is only trustworthy when checks_skipped contains no entry for that category. Final response (agent narrative): open with one sentence using `total_issues` from the summary (count of distinct issue types) and the output moment (e.g. '8 issues need attention before printing.'). Then emit up to 3 labelled sections in this order: **Critical Issues: N**, **Warnings: N**, and **Passed Checks** (no count \u2014 derived from absences of failed findings in scopes that were actually run; reference `checks_skipped` to avoid claiming a check passed when it was skipped). Skip **Critical Issues** or **Warnings** when N = 0. If Critical Issues and Warnings are both zero say 'Your file is production ready. No issues found.' and still include the Passed Checks section listing what was verified. Each finding (and each Passed Checks entry) is a single numbered line: N. {display_name} \u2014 {one-line action or consequence, or for Passed Checks: one-line confirmation of what passed}. No sub-lists. No bold heading per finding. No emojis. Restart numbering at 1 in Warnings and Passed Checks. Do NOT replace the numbered-list-of-findings with a single markdown table whose rows are the findings themselves; a per-finding affected-objects table as supplementary detail beneath a numbered finding line is OK, but the numbered line is required. Severity classification is principle-based (output moment + missing/render-breaking vs context-dependent) per the system prompt's PREFLIGHT SEVERITY policy \u2014 do not consult a static issue-to-tier table. Does not cover vision-only checks (contrast, visual alignment) or checks needing export-format context (transparency flattening). Use this for ANY readiness/verification request \u2014 prompts phrased with check / inspect / audit / verify / validate against document attributes (color mode, profile, bleed, resolution, units, artboard sizes, hidden or locked layers within export-range artboards, fonts, linked images, embedded images, text sizing/overset, spot colors, hairline strokes, overprint, transparency), as well as bare 'check this' / 'run a preflight' / 'production QA' / 'is it ready for print|export|share' / 'verify ... for print output'.",

327ef7         {
327f01           "name": "RecolorArtwork",
327f25           "description": "Remaps the color palette of vector artwork objects as a group using a Firefly-generated palette driven by a theme or mood prompt \u2014 use for palette, mood, tone, aesthetic, or style changes (e.g. 'make it warmer', 'recolor to autumn', 'more vibrant', 'ocean-like', 'cyberpunk neon'). Extracts prominent colors across all objects and remaps them together, preserving visual relationships. Use only for open-ended mood/theme changes: it generates a palette and cannot hit an exact value. If the request names the literal color(s) to end up with \u2014 a hex, a Pantone/spot name, 'all red', or a color space like 'grayscale'/'black and white' \u2014 use SetAppearance instead. The response's resultingColors field lists the distinct new colors the engine produced and applied (across all recolored objects, symbol instances and mesh nodes included). It is the authoritative record of the applied colors: read it to judge whether the palette matches the request, not what you expected. Do NOT capture a preview to check or second-guess the colors \u2014 a preview cannot report them more accurately than resultingColors and, being low-resolution, will often report them wrong (e.g. a deep navy read as a lighter blue), which only misleads you into a needless read-back or re-color. If resultingColorsOmitted is present, that many more colors were applied but dropped for compactness \u2014 judge the palette from the list, but don't call it the complete set. Requires network and consumes Firefly credits, so calls are expensive \u2014 minimize them. Output is generative and varies between calls. Before the first call, if the request gives no usable direction at all \u2014 no mood, hue, reference, or example to build a stylePrompt from (e.g. 'make it look nicer', 'change the colors') \u2014 ask the user what look or mood they want instead of guessing; a vague request won't be fixed by retrying, only by asking. HARD LIMIT: at most two calls per distinct recolor target, counting both successes and failures. This budget is per target, not per message: a message asking for several independent recolors (e.g. 'give the sky an autumn palette and make the mountains more vibrant') gets two calls for each. After a successful call for a target, stop calling for that target. Make a second call ONLY if resultingColors conflict with the request \u2014 wrong hue family, wrong temperature, or a named color missing (e.g. asked for warm autumn but got greens, or asked for navy but no blue appears). A result that satisfies the request is done; do NOT re-call to make it richer, more vibrant, add variety, or adjust one object. When a second call is warranted, name the exact hues or temperature to correct (e.g. 'warm oranges and reds, not green') and add guideColors for any named color the first call missed \u2014 do not just rephrase. If two calls still conflict with the request, ask the user for clearer direction rather than calling a third time. On failure the error message states the reason and whether a retry is worthwhile \u2014 relay the reason to the user and follow that guidance. A failure the message calls structural won't succeed on the same objects (prefer SetAppearance, or stop); a transient or input failure (e.g. too many UUIDs \u2014 hard cap is 5000, malformed input) is worth one corrected retry. Never call a third time. Fully supported: paths, groups, plugin art (live paint, blends, envelopes), text frames, and symbol instances. Mesh gradients are recolored too, though their palette can be less predictable than flat colors. Applicability depends on the object's actual art type, not how it was created \u2014 a vector-format file imported as native paths/groups is fully supported; check the object's structure rather than assume based on its origin. Skipped (reported in the response): objects with no reachable vector color data \u2014 raster content and opaque placed-item containers \u2014 plus foreign objects, legacy text, graphs, and repeat/symmetry art (radial, grid, symmetry, concentric repeat). ",

32c997           "description": "Runs multiple registered MCP tools in order in a single call to reduce round trips and context size. Maximum 25 operations (synchronous, blocking main thread). Requires EnableAgenticBatchTooling. Nested ExecuteBatchTool and ExecuteTool are not allowed. Each step runs with the same document context as a normal tool call (including undo grouping when an undo group is active). Results are returned as a structured JSON array in the \\\"results\\\" field of the envelope: per-step tool name, index, success, and result or error. By default every step runs regardless of earlier failures (the per-step \\\"success\\\" flag tells you which ones failed). For ordered sequences where a later step depends on an earlier one (e.g. create layer \u2192 move art into it), pass stopOnFirstError=true to halt at the first failure \u2014 the response then contains only the steps that ran. For batches >25 tools or very expensive operations, use IRIS batch_tool_call_request instead (async, server-side cancellation support).\\n\\nExample usage pattern (Python-style pseudocode):\\n```\\nitems = SomeListTool(...)\\n# step N+1 \u2014 build batch from the persisted variable\\ninvocations = []\\nfor item in items[\\\"detail_key\\\"]:\\n    invocations.append({\\n        \\\"tool\\\": \\\"TargetTool\\\",\\n        \\\"arguments\\\": {\\n            \\\"param1\\\": item[\\\"field1\\\"],\\n            \\\"param2\\\": item[\\\"field2\\\"],\\n            # ... map each required arg from the item\\n        },\\n    })\\nExecuteBatchTool(operations=json.dumps(invocations))  # serialize \u2014 'operations' is a string\\n```\\n\\nResponse format \u2014 a structured envelope, no double-parsing needed:\\n  {\\\"success\\\": <bool>, \\\"results\\\": [ {...}, {...}, ... ], \\\"details\\\": <string>}\\n\\\"success\\\" is true only if every step succeeded \u2014 if envelope \\\"success\\\" is true, you can skip iterating \\\"results\\\" unless you need per-step output. \\\"results\\\" is already a JSON array (parsed), not a string. \\\"details\\\" is empty on the normal path; it is populated only when the batch itself threw (e.g. malformed operations array, invalid JSON) and carries a single error message describing the batch-level failure \u2014 in that case \\\"results\\\" will be empty. Each entry in \\\"results\\\" has: \\\"tool\\\" (string), \\\"index\\\" (int), \\\"ai_err\\\" (int, 0 = ok), \\\"success\\\" (bool), and either \\\"result\\\" (parsed JSON object from the inner tool) or \\\"result_raw\\\" (string, fallback when the inner response was not valid JSON). 335c80 Object type does not support in-place fill, stroke, or paint changes.
335cc6 Object is a symbol instance; fill, stroke, and paint properties can't be changed without breaking its link to the symbol.
335d40 Object is a mesh; only a fill color applies (per-vertex). Stroke, fill removal, and overprint don't apply to a mesh.
335db5 Fill, stroke, and related paint properties can't be changed directly on a symbol instance. A symbol instance is a linked copy of a symbol, so these properties are driven by the symbol itself, not the instance. The most reliable way to restyle just this object - without changing the shared symbol for other instances - is to break its link to the symbol, a destructive action that expands the instance into normal, editable artwork. First confirm with the user whether they want to break the link and restyle this object. If they agree, break the link and then apply the requested change. If the link can't be broken, explain to the user how to break a symbol link manually so they can do it themselves.
